As for my sleep, I always wake up feeling completely exhausted and wiped out no matter how much sleep I get, but I'm not really sure if my actual sleep patterns have changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate more on your sleep patterns, specifically if you've been sleeping more or less than usual over the last two years, and how that relates to your overall feeling of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No matter how many hours of sleep I get, I always wake up feeling completely wiped out. This chronic exhaustion makes me feel like I'm constantly moving through mud during my clinic shifts and night classes. Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. There's this constant gray cloud hanging over me, and I feel really hopeless, like I'm just stuck in this same job while everyone else moves on. I've even stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops because I'm just too drained to do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these feelings of sadness and hopelessness started, and have they been constant over the last couple of years or have you had any periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: They started a little over two years ago, right after I graduated high school and started working full-time at the clinic instead of going to college with my friends. Since then, the heavy feeling has been pretty constant almost every day, and I haven't really had any periods where I felt like my old self or felt any better.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvements in your mood or periods without these feelings of sadness and hopelessness for at least two months at any point over the last two years?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks or improvements like that at all. This heavy, gray cloud has been completely constant and steady ever since I graduated high school over two years ago. There hasn't been a time where I felt okay, even for a few weeks, let alone two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling significantly better or even euphoric, where you had increased energy or decreased need for sleep, that lasted for at least a few days, or has it been a steady, constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness the entire time?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had any periods where I felt euphoric, \"high,\" or full of sudden energy. It’s just been a steady, constant heavy feeling like a gray cloud for the last two years. Instead of needing less sleep, I consistently feel completely wiped out and tired.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements or episodes of feeling better. Can you think of any specific events, changes in your life, or times when your symptoms might have worsened or changed in any way, even if it seems minor?\n\nPatient: It really started right after high school when I had to work full-time at the clinic instead of going to college like my friends. My feelings of being stuck got worse when I saw their social media posts about campus life, and it became even harder to handle once I tried to juggle work with night classes.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started after high school and have been constant since, but have you noticed any subtle changes or fluctuations in the intensity of your feelings of sadness or hopelessness in relation to specific events, seasons, or times of the year?\n\nPatient: The heavy feeling is pretty constant and doesn't change with the seasons, but it definitely gets worse and feels emptier when I see my friends posting about their college lives and internships. Even when good things happen, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, it doesn't really lift my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: You've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements, but can you tell me if there have been any periods, even brief, where your symptoms seemed to worsen or if they've remained at a steady level the entire time?\n\nPatient: It has been a very steady, constant feeling the entire time. I haven't really had any sharp spikes where it got significantly worse, it's just been that same continuous gray cloud that hasn't lifted since it started.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness for over two years without significant improvements, which helps differentiate between a persistent depressive disorder and a major depressive disorder; can you confirm again if there have been any periods, even brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm that I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my normal self at all. That heavy, gray cloud feeling and my complete lack of energy have been completely constant and steady for the entire two years without any break or relief.\n\nDoctor: To confirm, you've experienced this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any periods of significant improvement or return to your normal self, and without any episodes of elevated mood or increased energy, is that correct?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's completely correct. This heavy, gray feeling and hopelessness have been constant for over two years now without any real breaks or times where I felt like my old self. I've also never had any periods of high energy or \"hyper\" moods—it's just been a continuous baseline of feeling drained and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if you have experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the last two years, and if so, have they been consistent with your overall feeling of sadness and hopelessness?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major changes in my appetite over this time. As for my sleep, I always wake up feeling completely exhausted and wiped out no matter how much sleep I get, but I'm not really sure if my actual sleep patterns have changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate more on your sleep patterns, specifically if you've been sleeping more or less than usual over the last two years, and how that relates to your overall feeling of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No matter how many hours of sleep I get, I always wake up feeling completely wiped out. This chronic exhaustion makes me feel like I'm constantly moving through mud during my clinic shifts and night classes. Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. Even if something good happens, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, I just feel kind of empty.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there have been any subtle differences or variations in the quality of your depressive symptoms over the two years, such as differences in the intensity of hopelessness versus sadness, or have they remained relatively consistent in nature?\n\nPatient: They have been very consistent and steady over the last two years. It feels like a constant, heavy gray cloud that doesn't really lift, where both the empty feeling and the feeling of being stuck are always there together rather than shifting in intensity."

Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. Even if something good happens, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, I just feel kind of empty.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there have been any subtle differences or variations in the quality of your depressive symptoms over the two years, such as differences in the intensity of hopelessness versus sadness, or have they remained relatively consistent in nature?\n\nPatient: They have been very consistent and steady over the last two years. It feels like a constant, heavy gray cloud that doesn't really lift, where both the empty feeling and the feeling of being stuck are always there together rather than shifting in intensity.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you've lost interest in activities you once enjoyed, such as playing guitar and shooting hoops, but are there any small tasks or daily routines that you still find some pleasure or satisfaction in, even if it's minimal?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I still go through some of my daily routines like evening DIY projects or solitary gaming, but I don't get any real pleasure or satisfaction from them anymore. When I'm doing them, or even when something positive happens at work, I just feel numb and empty, like I'm simply going through the motions.

Can you think of any specific events, changes in your life, or times when your symptoms might have worsened or changed in any way, even if it seems minor?\n\nPatient: It really started right after high school when I had to work full-time at the clinic instead of going to college like my friends. My feelings of being stuck got worse when I saw their social media posts about campus life, and it became even harder to handle once I tried to juggle work with night classes.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started after high school and have been constant since, but have you noticed any subtle changes or fluctuations in the intensity of your feelings of sadness or hopelessness in relation to specific events, seasons, or times of the year?\n\nPatient: The heavy feeling is pretty constant and doesn't change with the seasons, but it definitely gets worse and feels emptier when I see my friends posting about their college lives and internships. Even when good things happen, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, it doesn't really lift my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: You've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements, but can you tell me if there have been any periods, even brief, where your symptoms seemed to worsen or if they've remained at a steady level the entire time?\n\nPatient: It has been a very steady, constant feeling the entire time. I haven't really had any sharp spikes where it got significantly worse, it's just been that same continuous gray cloud that hasn't lifted since it started.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness for over two years without significant improvements, which helps differentiate between a persistent depressive disorder and a major depressive disorder; can you confirm again if there have been any periods, even brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm that I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my normal self at all. That heavy, gray cloud feeling and my complete lack of energy have been completely constant and steady for the entire two years without any break or relief.\n\nDoctor: To confirm, you've experienced this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any periods of significant improvement or return to your normal self, and without any episodes of elevated mood or increased energy, is that correct?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's completely correct. This heavy, gray feeling and hopelessness have been constant for over two years now without any real breaks or times where I felt like my old self. I've also never had any periods of high energy or \"hyper\" moods—it's just been a continuous baseline of feeling drained and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if you have experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the last two years, and if so, have they been consistent with your overall feeling of sadness and hopelessness?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major changes in my appetite over this time. As for my sleep, I always wake up feeling completely exhausted and wiped out no matter how much sleep I get, but I'm not really sure if my actual sleep patterns have changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate more on your sleep patterns, specifically if you've been sleeping more or less than usual over the last two years, and how that relates to your overall feeling of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No matter how many hours of sleep I get, I always wake up feeling completely wiped out. This chronic exhaustion makes me feel like I'm constantly moving through mud during my clinic shifts and night classes. Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. Even if something good happens, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, I just feel kind of empty.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there have been any subtle differences or variations in the quality of your depressive symptoms over the two years, such as differences in the intensity of hopelessness versus sadness, or have they remained relatively consistent in nature?\n\nPatient: They have been very consistent and steady over the last two years. It feels like a constant, heavy gray cloud that doesn't really lift, where both the empty feeling and the feeling of being stuck are always there together rather than shifting in intensity.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you've lost interest in activities you once enjoyed, such as playing guitar and shooting hoops, but are there any small tasks or daily routines that you still find some pleasure or satisfaction in, even if it's minimal?\n\nPatient: I still go through some of my daily routines like evening DIY projects or solitary gaming, but I don't get any real pleasure or satisfaction from them anymore. When I'm doing them, or even when something positive happens at work, I just feel numb and empty, like I'm simply going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years, but can you confirm if this has been a single, continuous episode or if you've had any distinct periods where you felt somewhat better, even if briefly, before returning to this state?"
  }
]

Yes, it has definitely been one single, continuous episode. I haven't had any periods where I felt even slightly better or got a break from it. Even when positive things happen, that heavy, empty feeling doesn't lift, so it just feels like one long, unbroken stretch.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. There's this constant gray cloud hanging over me, and I feel really hopeless, like I'm just stuck in this same job while everyone else moves on. I've even stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops because I'm just too drained to do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these feelings of sadness and hopelessness started, and have they been constant over the last couple of years or have you had any periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: They started a little over two years ago, right after I graduated high school and started working full-time at the clinic instead of going to college with my friends. Since then, the heavy feeling has been pretty constant almost every day, and I haven't really had any periods where I felt like my old self or felt any better.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvements in your mood or periods without these feelings of sadness and hopelessness for at least two months at any point over the last two years?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks or improvements like that at all. This heavy, gray cloud has been completely constant and steady ever since I graduated high school over two years ago. There hasn't been a time where I felt okay, even for a few weeks, let alone two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of feeling significantly better or even euphoric, where you had increased energy or decreased need for sleep, that lasted for at least a few days, or has it been a steady, constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness the entire time?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had any periods where I felt euphoric, \"high,\" or full of sudden energy. It’s just been a steady, constant heavy feeling like a gray cloud for the last two years. Instead of needing less sleep, I consistently feel completely wiped out and tired.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements or episodes of feeling better. Can you think of any specific events, changes in your life, or times when your symptoms might have worsened or changed in any way, even if it seems minor?\n\nPatient: It really started right after high school when I had to work full-time at the clinic instead of going to college like my friends. My feelings of being stuck got worse when I saw their social media posts about campus life, and it became even harder to handle once I tried to juggle work with night classes.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started after high school and have been constant since, but have you noticed any subtle changes or fluctuations in the intensity of your feelings of sadness or hopelessness in relation to specific events, seasons, or times of the year?\n\nPatient: The heavy feeling is pretty constant and doesn't change with the seasons, but it definitely gets worse and feels emptier when I see my friends posting about their college lives and internships. Even when good things happen, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, it doesn't really lift my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: You've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements, but can you tell me if there have been any periods, even brief, where your symptoms seemed to worsen or if they've remained at a steady level the entire time?\n\nPatient: It has been a very steady, constant feeling the entire time. I haven't really had any sharp spikes where it got significantly worse, it's just been that same continuous gray cloud that hasn't lifted since it started.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness for over two years without significant improvements, which helps differentiate between a persistent depressive disorder and a major depressive disorder; can you confirm again if there have been any periods, even brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm that I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my normal self at all. That heavy, gray cloud feeling and my complete lack of energy have been completely constant and steady for the entire two years without any break or relief.\n\nDoctor: To confirm, you've experienced this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any periods of significant improvement or return to your normal self, and without any episodes of elevated mood or increased energy, is that correct?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's completely correct. This heavy, gray feeling and hopelessness have been constant for over two years now without any real breaks or times where I felt like my old self. I've also never had any periods of high energy or \"hyper\" moods—it's just been a continuous baseline of feeling drained and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if you have experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the last two years, and if so, have they been consistent with your overall feeling of sadness and hopelessness?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major changes in my appetite over this time. As for my sleep, I always wake up feeling completely exhausted and wiped out no matter how much sleep I get, but I'm not really sure if my actual sleep patterns have changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate more on your sleep patterns, specifically if you've been sleeping more or less than usual over the last two years, and how that relates to your overall feeling of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No matter how many hours of sleep I get, I always wake up feeling completely wiped out. This chronic exhaustion makes me feel like I'm constantly moving through mud during my clinic shifts and night classes. Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. Even if something good happens, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, I just feel kind of empty.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there have been any subtle differences or variations in the quality of your depressive symptoms over the two years, such as differences in the intensity of hopelessness versus sadness, or have they remained relatively consistent in nature?\n\nPatient: They have been very consistent and steady over the last two years. It feels like a constant, heavy gray cloud that doesn't really lift, where both the empty feeling and the feeling of being stuck are always there together rather than shifting in intensity.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you've lost interest in activities you once enjoyed, such as playing guitar and shooting hoops, but are there any small tasks or daily routines that you still find some pleasure or satisfaction in, even if it's minimal?\n\nPatient: I still go through some of my daily routines like evening DIY projects or solitary gaming, but I don't get any real pleasure or satisfaction from them anymore. When I'm doing them, or even when something positive happens at work, I just feel numb and empty, like I'm simply going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years, but can you confirm if this has been a single, continuous episode or if you've had any distinct periods where you felt somewhat better, even if briefly, before returning to this state?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has definitely been one single, continuous episode. I haven't had any periods where I felt even slightly better or got a break from it. Even when positive things happen, that heavy, empty feeling doesn't lift, so it just feels like one long, unbroken stretch.\n\nDoctor: To further clarify, you've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any breaks, but can you confirm if you've experienced any periods where your symptoms seemed to intensify or change in character, even if briefly, or have they remained consistent in nature throughout?\n\nPatient: It's kind of hard to say, but I haven't really noticed any sudden spikes or changes in how I feel. It has just been a constant, steady heavy feeling of being low and tired the entire time.\n\nDoctor: You've described a continuous episode of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without significant improvement, which suggests a persistent depressive disorder, but to differentiate between F32.9 and F33.1, can you tell me if you've experienced any significant life events or stressors that might have triggered or worsened your symptoms over this time?"

My feelings of being stuck got worse when I saw their social media posts about campus life, and it became even harder to handle once I tried to juggle work with night classes.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started after high school and have been constant since, but have you noticed any subtle changes or fluctuations in the intensity of your feelings of sadness or hopelessness in relation to specific events, seasons, or times of the year?\n\nPatient: The heavy feeling is pretty constant and doesn't change with the seasons, but it definitely gets worse and feels emptier when I see my friends posting about their college lives and internships. Even when good things happen, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, it doesn't really lift my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: You've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any significant improvements, but can you tell me if there have been any periods, even brief, where your symptoms seemed to worsen or if they've remained at a steady level the entire time?\n\nPatient: It has been a very steady, constant feeling the entire time. I haven't really had any sharp spikes where it got significantly worse, it's just been that same continuous gray cloud that hasn't lifted since it started.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness for over two years without significant improvements, which helps differentiate between a persistent depressive disorder and a major depressive disorder; can you confirm again if there have been any periods, even brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm that I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my normal self at all. That heavy, gray cloud feeling and my complete lack of energy have been completely constant and steady for the entire two years without any break or relief.\n\nDoctor: To confirm, you've experienced this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any periods of significant improvement or return to your normal self, and without any episodes of elevated mood or increased energy, is that correct?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's completely correct. This heavy, gray feeling and hopelessness have been constant for over two years now without any real breaks or times where I felt like my old self. I've also never had any periods of high energy or \"hyper\" moods—it's just been a continuous baseline of feeling drained and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if you have experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the last two years, and if so, have they been consistent with your overall feeling of sadness and hopelessness?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major changes in my appetite over this time. As for my sleep, I always wake up feeling completely exhausted and wiped out no matter how much sleep I get, but I'm not really sure if my actual sleep patterns have changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate more on your sleep patterns, specifically if you've been sleeping more or less than usual over the last two years, and how that relates to your overall feeling of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No matter how many hours of sleep I get, I always wake up feeling completely wiped out. This chronic exhaustion makes me feel like I'm constantly moving through mud during my clinic shifts and night classes. Because of that, I have absolutely no energy left for things I used to enjoy, like playing guitar or shooting hoops.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped playing guitar and shooting hoops due to feeling drained, but have you noticed any activities, even small ones, that still bring you some joy or interest, or has the lack of pleasure been complete across all activities?\n\nPatient: The lack of pleasure feels pretty complete, to be honest. Even when I try to do things like DIY projects, gaming, or playing guitar, there’s just no spark or joy in them anymore. Even if something good happens, like a patient thanking me at the clinic, I just feel kind of empty.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if there have been any subtle differences or variations in the quality of your depressive symptoms over the two years, such as differences in the intensity of hopelessness versus sadness, or have they remained relatively consistent in nature?\n\nPatient: They have been very consistent and steady over the last two years. It feels like a constant, heavy gray cloud that doesn't really lift, where both the empty feeling and the feeling of being stuck are always there together rather than shifting in intensity.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you've lost interest in activities you once enjoyed, such as playing guitar and shooting hoops, but are there any small tasks or daily routines that you still find some pleasure or satisfaction in, even if it's minimal?\n\nPatient: I still go through some of my daily routines like evening DIY projects or solitary gaming, but I don't get any real pleasure or satisfaction from them anymore. When I'm doing them, or even when something positive happens at work, I just feel numb and empty, like I'm simply going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years, but can you confirm if this has been a single, continuous episode or if you've had any distinct periods where you felt somewhat better, even if briefly, before returning to this state?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has definitely been one single, continuous episode. I haven't had any periods where I felt even slightly better or got a break from it. Even when positive things happen, that heavy, empty feeling doesn't lift, so it just feels like one long, unbroken stretch.\n\nDoctor: To further clarify, you've had this constant feeling of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without any breaks, but can you confirm if you've experienced any periods where your symptoms seemed to intensify or change in character, even if briefly, or have they remained consistent in nature throughout?\n\nPatient: It's kind of hard to say, but I haven't really noticed any sudden spikes or changes in how I feel. It has just been a constant, steady heavy feeling of being low and tired the entire time.\n\nDoctor: You've described a continuous episode of sadness and hopelessness for over two years without significant improvement, which suggests a persistent depressive disorder, but to differentiate between F32.9 and F33.1, can you tell me if you've experienced any significant life events or stressors that might have triggered or worsened your symptoms over this time?\n\nPatient: It all started right after high school when I had to start working full-time as a medical assistant instead of going to college with my friends, due to financial reasons and family expectations. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
